@@15684115 <h> Study : Biggest tornado outbreaks are spawning more twisters <p> WASHINGTON ( AP ) " The most extreme tornado outbreaks are mysteriously spawning many more twisters than they did decades ago , a new study claimed . <p> The **27;530;TOOLONG outbreak that might have involved 12 tornadoes 50 years ago now has on average about 20 , said Columbia University applied physics professor Michael Tippett , lead author of the study in Thursday 's journal Science . <p> The study comes in the end of a year that has been on track to have the fewest tornadoes on record , but is also on the heels of the outbreak Tuesday night and Wednesday morning that killed five people and injured at least 46 in Alabama and Tennessee " precisely the kind of outbreak Tippett studied . <p> As of now , there were 36 tornado reports Tuesday in Alabama , Tennessee , Mississippi and Louisiana , half of them were the stronger type Tippett studied , said meteorologist Patrick Marsh of the National Weather Service 's Storm Prediction Center in Norman , Oklahoma . <p> Tippett and colleagues @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ above the minimal wind rating and found a steady uptick in the biggest outbreaks since the mid-1960s . <p> " Something 's up , " Tippett said . " The tornadoes that do occur are occurring in clusters . It 's not any increase in the ( total ) number of tornadoes . " <p> Marsh said until Tuesday 's outbreak , there had been 830 tornadoes all year , which was below the previous low for that date of 920 . The normal number through late November is closer to 1,300 . While 2011 was one of the busiest tornado years for the size of twisters that Tippett studied , 2012 was one of the quietest , he said . <p> Unlike other spikes of extreme weather in recent years , Tippett and colleagues could not find the fingerprints of man-made global warming in the change . <p> " It 's not what we expected , " Tippett said . " Either it 's not climate change because not everything is , or it is some aspect of climate change we do n't understand yet . " <h> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the detection in the change of frequency of occurrence of these events , " Stanford University professor Noah Diffenbaugh said . <p> But some of the other scientists said improved reporting and urban sprawl increases the number of recent tornadoes and negates some of the trend Tippett found . They also disagree with the particular type of measurements that Tippett used and some of his definitions . <p> " It 's a useful exercise , " said Oklahoma University meteorology professor Howard Bluestein , " but I would be very , very careful in accepting it . @@15686115 <h> Gordon Campbell on Anne Tolley 's callous folly <h> Gordon Campbell on Anne Tolley 's callous folly <p> Years ago , I remember someone in the Heath Ministry telling me off the record that regulatory oversight in this country largely consisted in ' waiting for something to turn green or fall off somebody ' before the authorities would swing into action . Prevention is often deemed as being too costly , or too intrusive on business-as-usual . The ongoing failures in monitoring the fishing industry 's snapper catch -- ie onboard cameras that fail , monitoring that has been delegated by the authorities to a firm owned by the fishing industry -- are the all too typical outcome . <p> Only some of the contributing factors are structural . Yes , in a country as small as New Zealand , it can be difficult to devise truly independent systems of oversight and review . Everyone tends to know everyone , or has worked for them in some capacity or -- all too often -- they will be being paid by the same ministry or industry they are being @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the language of discretion . There is an art form in drawing up terms of reference for an inquiry so that the powerful will be safely insulated from the potential for political fallout . <p> As a " she 'll be right " nation , we 're not hard-wired to see the necessity for regulatory oversight , either . After all , our Parliament runs on a single chamber with no written Constitution , a simple majority of MPs can pass sweeping laws , and our courts do n't need to be reminded of the supremacy of Parliament . In addition , a climate of cost-cutting within government can readily work against full and fearless investigations of the state 's failings , especially if the victims are eligible for compensation . <p> Last week 's conflict between Social Development Minister Anne Tolley and District Court judge Carolyn Henwood illustrated quite a few of the flaws in the system . As chair of the Confidential Listening and Assistance Service ( CLAS ) panel , Henwood has heard testimony from over 1,100 people who were abused in state care between the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of Social Welfare . During that time , more than 100,000 children were reportedly taken from their parents , and placed in state institutions . Significant numbers of them were seriously abused . <p> After a patient , below-the radar investigation stretching over several years , Henwood and the CLAS panel recommended that -- among other things -- an independent public inquiry was required into the processes that enabled this abuse , and also into its ongoing impact ; and that meanwhile , the state should issue a general apology for the systemic failures revealed to date . <p> Tolley has refused on both counts . She has rejected the need ( or desirability at this late stage ) for a public inquiry fully independent of the state agency under the spotlight . No public apology was deemed appropriate because -- in Tolley 's view -- no systemic failure had occurred , given that only 3.5% of former state wards had complained of abuse . <p> As Hill also indicated , some respondents might well have had misgivings about confiding their experiences to the very agency that had previously @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ percentage of abuse claims seemed particularly mean-spirited . Only a small percentage of Catholic children have ever laid formal claims for compensation for abuses committed by Catholic priests , either . Yet surely , Tolley would n't attempt to deny that systemic failings have occurred within that particular institution . <p> Reportedly , a public inquiry could result in the government being liable for major compensation claims -- a risk mentioned in the papers that Tolley took to Cabinet - and this prospect may also have been a factor in Tolley 's decisions . ( Well , Treaty claims for past wrongs by the state are being compensated , finally . Arguably , so should these failings . ) So far , this shabby episode has done nothing to re-assure the public that Henwood 's findings -- and the victims - have been handled fairly . As Henwood has indicated , by refusing to fully investigate how this widespread abuse could have been committed against so many vulnerable children for so long , we are not doing all we can to ensure that it wo n't happen again . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ on the Putin article the other day . Believe me , that article was n't meant to perpetuate a new Red Scare . Personally , I 'm a sceptic when it comes to the imperial actions of both Washington and Moscow . What I was arguing in that article was that compromise does n't seem to be part of the approach to dispute resolution of the current and imminent leaders of the US , Russia , or China . This convergence of alpha male winner-take-all tendencies is IMO , reason for concern . <p> Obviously , there are any number of reasons to oppose Donald Trump , even if many of his supporters have been victims of decades of neo-liberalism . ( That does n't excuse the racism , or the misogyny . ) For similar reasons , Putin 's kleptocratic tendencies and use of reactionary religious and right wing forces to suppress dissent make him no friend of the left , either . IMO , these guys are virtual mirror images of each other . It is not a matter of lining up on one side or the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 'd argue that a Trump/Putin duopoly would be likely to be to the detriment of nearly everyone else -- including on policies about climate change , although the US under Trump is a bigger problem there . To win back the white working class , any major changes on immigration and identity politics would violate the Democrats ' traditional commitments on social justice , and would alienate support among Hispanics and blacks . Pandering is n't an option . <p> That said , did Putin -- and Trump 's declared admiration for him -- decide the outcome of the US election ? Given the narrowness of the victory margin in the Electoral College states , any number of factors could have been decisive . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have cited Putin 's role as being decisive in the way that I did . With hindsight , I agree . <p> What I should have said instead is that most of the developments working in Putin 's favour -- the rise of Francois Fillon in France , of Trump in the US and of the populist right throughout Europe -- have virtually fallen into his lap , rather than being the fruits of his active design . The smoke involved ie , the prior lobbying by Trump campaign manager Paul Manafort for the pro-Russian regime in Ukraine , the paid work by the new national security adviser Michael Flynn for the Russians , and even the hacking of the DNC etc - should have been taken as reflecting an affinity between Trump and Putin , and not as evidence of Putin 's direct manipulation . Saying so is not to deny Russia 's rationale for regarding Ukraine as a vital buffer zone for its own defence , which I 've previously acknowledged . It is n't a binary world . <p> While most people agree that increased sugar consumption is a major cause of too many New Zealanders being overweight and obese , what we should do about this remains a matter of debate and argument . More&gt;&gt; <p> Safe to say that no-one , but no-one has had a better 2016 than Vladimir Putin . What an annus mirabilis it has been for him . @@15700391 <h> Acting on carbon : What youth has to say <p> Francesca Bradley , from left , Pubudu Senanayake , Chris Stinson and Rosalee Jenkin all members of the Christchurch " Generation Zero " organisation dedicated to tackling climate change which includes a Zero Carbon Act that they hope to present to the government . <p> Transport is the natural focus of local climate change action . Cycle rally on the Auckland waterfront . <p> MICHAEL FIELD/FAIRFAX NZ <p> Auckland 's Occupy Aotea Square protest in 2011 : Playing out the ritual of confrontation . <p> WARWICK SMITH/FAIRFAX NZ <p> What 's the message here ? Generation Zero inflates a dinosaur telling Government to ' get with the times ' . <h> Relevant offers <p> Do the young spend too much time " buggerising around on Facebook " to be politically effective ? Generation Zero is moving beyond street stunts to engage with real climate change politics , as JOHN McCRONE reports . <p> Look at who still runs the world . Donald Trump is 70 . If Hillary Clinton had made it as US president , she would @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of the millennials , is 75 . <p> What gives ? Where is the voice of youth these days ? On climate change especially -- and what could matter more than the state of the planet they will inherit ? -- the young seem politically helpless and sidelined . <p> Trump , born 1946 , seems the definition of the baby boom generation managing to cling on to power in self-interested fashion . <p> He has already threatened to pull the United States out of last year 's Paris climate agreement and is busy appointing " climate contrarians " like conservative think-tanker Myron Ebell to his inner circle . <p> Meanwhile the twenty-somethings -- to borrow Gerry Brownlee 's classic put-down -- appear to be mostly carping , moaning , and buggerising around on Facebook . <p> In New Zealand , the face of youth climate activism is Generation Zero , formed in 2011 as a permanent organisation for 18 to 30 year olds following the sending of several youth delegations to the annual United Nations COP ( conference of the parties ) climate negotiations . <p> But is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to be able to cut through and force the old to take their future more seriously ? <p> Publicly , the group is known mostly for the occasional headline-grabbing stunt . There was the flash mob underwear protest when 40 members surprised commuters on the morning train from Ngauranga to Wellington by stripping down to their scanties . <p> " We were aiming to expose the government 's unbalanced transport budget and what better way to do that than by exposing ourselves , " said the 22-year-old Generation Zero organiser . <p> Ah , remember when flash mob videos on social media were a thing ? <p> There have been plenty more such events -- mostly less well covered probably because they involved more actual bodily cover up . The Christchurch branch of Generation Zero turned out with a boombox and dressed in 1980s Lycra workout gear to agitate for bus lanes in Riccarton Rd . <p> The Auckland branch had its " bike to the future " in Karangahape Rd where hundreds wore white -- to symbolise a road marking -- and rode in formation in support of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ frivolous . Is this the angriest the young can get ? Or does the millennial generation just have a different idea about what political approach will work in this social media age ? <p> ? ? ? <p> To find out , I gatecrash the fortnightly committee meeting for Christchurch 's Generation Zero . It seems an opportune time as COP 22 has been taking place in Marrakesh -- surely the highpoint of the climate activist 's calendar ? <p> Yet tonight there are only four in the lobby of Canterbury University 's history department . Rosalee Jenkin , Pubudu Senanayake , Francesca Bradley and Chris Stinson . <p> Senanayake , a physics PhD who now works for Statistics NZ , apologises . " Usually we have a core group of seven to eight . But this evening there 's also the Singularity Summit in Christchurch . A few of our people are there . " <p> An even cooler event to talk about the infinite possibilities of technology . <p> Of course with revolutions , the size of the revolutionary committees is not the issue . Small sparks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ puzzles I have as a grey-haired outsider wondering what counts as effective youth politics . <p> First , why an activism that seems so domestic and cheerful -- the transport policy focused stunts ? Do n't you guys want to shake a fist at the world , or at least John Key and his crew , I ask ? <p> And then is n't it all a little -- lazy ? These days it appears to be about a belief that social media creates a platform for creating political change . As with the Arab Spring or Occupy Wall Street , there is a view that the establishment can now be brought down by a cell phone and judiciously aimed Tweet . <p> But oh . Arab Spring and Occupy seemed to change very little . Instead , we have just had the rise of Trump as the best example of how social media can be used . <p> The group laughs . Already my thesis is sounding muddled . <p> Senanayake says , wind it back . What is Generation Zero trying to do ? <p> Its philosophy @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ action should concentrate on what it would mean for New Zealand to live up to a commitment of being a zero carbon nation -- halting the constant growth of emissions in its tracks . <p> And that , for example , makes urban transport central . New Zealand is particularly un-green in its reliance on cars . <p> " There 's a vision of a world where climate change has actually been dealt with . But it 's really hard for people to engage with . So part of what we are doing is trying to make that link -- show how better public transport , or active modes of transport , are a step to that world , " Senanayake says . <p> So it is a conscious policy of starting with what people can hope to change in their own political backyard . <p> Jenkin , a fine arts student now working as a digital archivist , adds this local focus also boosts morale and helps with recruitment . <p> She says life would be bleak if you obsessed about the start-stop progress of international climate talks @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ me personally , continuing to act is the only option because otherwise I would just fall into a horrible pit of despair . " <p> And it helps bring new members in because many people might join because cycleways are their thing , and climate change activism becomes a natural extension of that . <p> Then upbeat does n't mean lightweight . <p> Jenkin says the stunts are aimed at creating public awareness . But the meat of Generation Zero 's work is actually helping the young connect with the normal policy processes of government . A lot of the members ' volunteer time is spent very boringly on raising submissions on local authority plans . <p> That was why the Christchurch branch of Generation Zero was in Riccarton Rd , rallying support for council proposals to install bus lanes despite the stiff opposition of business-owners along the route . <p> And why more recently it campaigned in favour of a council plan to restrict cars through Victoria St under its Accessible City policy . <p> The shopkeepers won that one , says Jenkin . Or at least the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ new idea and the council was just a bit timid I think . " <p> So Generation Zero has in fact been learning to play the conventional political game . <p> Senanayake says an early realisation was that petitions only count as a single name when making submissions to council . The group had to switch to mobilising people to write individual letters to demonstrate a weight of public opinion . <p> " What we do now is boil an issue down so there are four or five bullet points that we can give people to think about . " <p> Jenkin says what was great about the " let 's get moving " boombox stunt in Riccarton Rd was it was also an opportunity to gather such submissions . <p> " We were rocking out to 80s ' music for two hours . We had so much fun . And we had a table set up so every time someone walked past , we 'd dance up and ask if they wanted to write a submission on the spot . We got about 30 people doing that . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ build a mandate to speak for climate change within the political process . The Auckland branch already has the council there nervous enough that councillors respond even to the mention it might be about to campaign on something . <p> So it is about gradually developing clout as a respectable lobby group rather than hitting the streets as an " angry , placard-waving , potentially violent , mob " , Jenkin says . <p> Stinson , a chemistry student , agrees . " There 're other groups that do that kind of direct action really well . But by being deliberately less ' protesty ' -- making sure we 've done our homework really well , and working at a policy level -- we can get into doors that other organisations ca n't . " <p> Bradley , a project manager , says that is also why Generation Zero does n't get down about a lack of instant change . It is in it for the long haul . <p> The Victoria St decision did n't go its way . " But whenever there 's a setback , we @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ into another opportunity . " <p> ? ? ? <p> It is making much more sense . There is real work in challenging local plans . <p> Ahead of the recent local government elections , Generation Zero also created an online rating of the climate credentials of all the candidates -- a guide on how to vote , as politicians react to what might affect their own futures . <p> It intends to be a substantial force . Generation Zero gets some funding from the Quakers and the Tindall Foundation . It might be able to afford a national co-ordinator by next year . <p> This brings up the issue of how great it must be that millennial activists at least have the advantage of being digital natives . Everyone has their phone or laptop on the go at the monthly catch-up . <p> No longer the need to march the streets or knock on doors anymore . You can sit on a couch and spread your revolution via Facebook . <p> The group groans . Senanayake says that has to be the biggest misconception . <p> Modern activists @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ across in two worlds . And the social media one is changing so fast that even twenty-somethings are struggling to keep up . <p> " It 's weirdly like running a start-up . You have to keep iterating because something that worked really well in 2014 might not work so well now . " <p> Stinson chimes in . It is exactly right about flash mobs , he says . " If something works , everyone jumps on the bandwagon and you just get drowned out . You 've got to constantly be coming up with something new . " <p> Nothing is ever as easy as it seems . Post-mortem analysis of why the Occupy movement came and went without changing anything much is that all it managed to do was set street protests against police forces designed to absorb their actions . <p> It became more about a familiar ritual which left the politicians insulated and the crowds -- watching themselves through the magnifying lens of social media -- the spectators of their own protest . <p> Jenkin says she remembers the Occupy encampment in Hagley Park @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ while the internet is good for organising a group or Googling for information -- modern activists have it much better in that regard , the world is at their fingertips -- Stinson says you ca n't simply speak the truth on Instagram or Twitter and expect it to change the world by going viral . <p> Stinson says just in the past couple of years , with Facebook and other social media going " algorithmic " , the ground has shifted under activism yet again . <p> It used to be that Facebook seemed to reach people in open-minded fashion . For a while , it was a broadcast medium . <p> But Stinson says Facebook now filters traffic to direct people towards what they already seem to find most interesting . Instead of opening up the conversations , it is closing them down . <p> This is what happened with the US election , he says . " It divided into the Blue and Red feed . " People only saw the stories that best fitted their prejudices . <p> Senanayake chips in . " Even in 2014 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of society . Now the algorithms have improved to such an extent , you end up canvassing a bubble . " <p> Jenkin says it is an arms race . Generation Zero is increasingly having to pay to boost posts in a bid to break out of the tight friend circles . " We have to put money on things we really want to get out to people , like something urgent that needs submissions . " <p> Senanayake says he believes millennial activists have to get back to more old fashioned face-to-face encounters . He goes out at lunchtimes now to talk about climate change with the construction workers in central Christchurch . <p> So no , social media is not the secret weapon of the young . " You 're having to spend more time doing the online stuff as that gets harder to do . And then you have to be out there in the streets as well , " Jenkin says . <p> ? ? ? <p> Generation Zero turns out to be a traditional organisation in many ways . It sees the value of @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ <p> But as well as playing that game , it wants to be a bridge that connects youth to the actual machinery of government . And to do that , it has to develop the mana of representing real people . <p> Next year , ahead of the general election , Generation Zero hopes to mount a rather more meaningful action at the national level . <p> Paul Young , Wellington-based co-founder and policy director of Generation Zero , says the group is going to draft a Zero Carbon Act -- a legally-binding framework that , if enacted , would force the New Zealand Government to follow policies which would completely eliminate fossil fuel carbon emissions by 2050 . <p> " We 're basing it on the 2008 UK Climate Change Act , " Young says . The British Parliament accepted just such a long-term restriction on politicians to prevent the kind of back-sliding that New Zealand has seen over its emissions trading scheme , for instance . <p> It was a bold move in the UK . Although the act ended up being not too controversial during its @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Targets were easy to meet because economic activity cooled . <p> But anyway , says Young , the plan is to promote a bill that would show New Zealand what it would be like to take climate change seriously -- lock in the desires of youth in a way that the baby boomers in charge ca n't keep ignoring . <p> " The idea 's been a bit under the radar for a while , but we 're hoping we can really make some noise around it next year . " <p> It is still a world where legislation counts for more than any number of Tweets . So drumming up a groundswell of support for a Zero Carbon Act would be a highly effective way of millennials achieving real political change . 
@@15703901 <h> Four major cities move to ban diesel vehicles <p> The leaders of four major global cities have vowed to stop the use of all diesel-powered cars and trucks by the middle of the next decade . <p> The domes of Les Invalides , left , and the Pantheon in Paris can be seen through a veil of smog . Photo : AFP <p> The mayors of Paris , Mexico City , Madrid and Athens say they are implementing the ban to improve air quality . <p> They say they will give incentives for alternative vehicle use and promote walking and cycling . <p> The commitments were made in Mexico at a biennial meeting of city leaders . <p> The use of diesel in transport has come under increasing scrutiny in recent years , as concerns about its impact on air quality have grown . The World Health Organization ( WHO ) says that around 3 million deaths every year are linked to exposure to outdoor air pollution . <p> Diesel engines contribute to the problem in two key ways - through the production of particulate matter ( PM @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ PM can penetrate the lungs and can contribute to cardiovascular illness and death . <p> Nitrogen oxides can help form ground level ozone and this can exacerbate breathing difficulties , even for people without a history of respiratory problems . <p> As the evidence has mounted , environmental groups have used the courts to try and enforce clear air standards and regulations . In the UK , campaigners have recently had success in forcing the government to act more quickly . <p> Now , mayors from a number of major cities with well known air quality problems have decided to use their authority to clamp down on the use of diesel . <p> In the UK , campaigners are calling for London 's mayor to commit to phase out diesel vehicles from London by 2025 . <p> Mayor Sadiq Khan has proposed an expansion to the planned Ultra-Low Emission Zone in central London . <p> ClientEarth lawyer Alan Andrews said : " In the UK , London 's mayor is considering bolder action than his predecessor , proposing an expansion to the planned Ultra-Low Emission Zone . This is @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ " <p> Heavy traffic is seen in Mexico City . Photo : AFP <p> At the C40 meeting of urban leaders in Mexico , the four mayors declared that they would ban all diesel vehicles by 2025 and " commit to doing everything in their power to incentivise the use of electric , hydrogen and hybrid vehicles " . <p> " It is no secret that in Mexico City , we grapple with the twin problems of air pollution and traffic , " said the city 's mayor , Miguel ? ngel Mancera . <p> " By expanding alternative transportation options like our Bus Rapid Transport and subway systems , while also investing in cycling infrastructure , we are working to ease congestion in our roadways and our lungs . " <p> Paris has already taken a series of steps to cut the impact of diesel cars and trucks . @@15707640 <h> Related Content <p> This leads to greater dependency on the water from the dam , and when water becomes scarce , farmers are at even more risk because they then have more animals and crops . Inevitably , they become less resilient . <p> The Opihi case study <p> An unfortunate example of this is the Opihi irrigation dam in South Canterbury . <p> To pay for the water farmers intensified so water consumption went up . Meanwhile , rainfall and snowfall decreased so , in the past few years , there has not been enough water to fill the dam and keep water flowing in the river and to farmers . <p> As predicted , the water quality in rivers downstream has declined dramatically ; the community and farmers have lost out . <p> In reality , the only way a dam could possibly mitigate climate change would be if it did not lead to changed land-use or water-use , and the water was not touched until needed to mitigate a drought . <p> The second claim is there are ecological gains to be had from dams @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be around the vague notion of " flushing flows " . But , like the toilet , you only need flush it when you have created the need . <p> Intensifying farming means diffuse nutrient loss , leading to algal blooms . Algal blooms kill fish and other life in the river and make it unsafe to swim . So any need to flush is driven by intensification . <p> Dams drive intensification so it 's a completely circular argument . Damming a river also removes any natural flushing flows so the idea that it is a gain to take away the natural flow variability and replace it with an artificial one is just daft . <p> Unlike the few paragraphs needed to unravel the loose thinking of dam salespeople , thousands of words would be required to detail the long list of negative ecological impacts . <p> So , instead , let me point to a recent peer-reviewed scientific paper . It was published in a top freshwater journal that reviewed 165 scientific papers on dam impacts and found that 92 per cent of them reported declining or @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ countries are recognising these negative effects . The United States , for example , has stopped building new dams and now dams are being removed from many rivers . <p> Large-scale dams are a folly because large-scale water storage locks us all in to a high-risk , high-cost , high-impact system . <p> The way to grow food for the future is farm-scale , on-farm water retention and there are already many proven ways to achieve this . <p> New Zealand agriculture must be based on clean , healthy sustainable farming that matches land use to climate . Maintaining a clean , healthy , sustainable agricultural image is the best value-add our produce can have . <p> Dr Mike Joy is a freshwater ecologist at Massey University 's Institute of Agriculture and Environment .

@@15730186 <h> Antarctic starfish may show climate change will produce winners and losers <p> WILL HARVIE <p> Last updated 05:00 , December 5 2016 <p> ANTARCTICA NZ 2016-17 <p> Antarctic starfish are " abundant , they travel well , they 're happy in the lab and we 've got lots of experience with them " , says the University of Otago 's Miles Lamare . Unfortunately , " it 's a one-way ticket " . <p> Miles Lamare , left , from the University of Otago and Antonio Garcia from the University of Brussels examine starfish harvested from McMurdo Sound . <p> The most common species near Antarctica New Zealand 's Scott Base are omnivorous scavengers that feed on primarily seal carcasses and faeces . About 10cm across when adult , Odontaster validus can live up to 100 years and can survive as deep as 600m . <p> They 're also a useful species for climate change researchers . That 's because Antarctic starfish will have to adapt to changing conditions and it 's possible to model those conditions in a lab , something impossible using larger and more mobile @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ professor Miles Lamare and colleagues captured 600 starfish from the Ross Sea . They will rear them in refrigerated tanks over the next two years in Dunedin to monitor their adaptability to climate change . <p> ANTARCTICA NZ 2016-17 <p> Starfish are harvested using a remote underwater vehicle from a hut on ice near Antarctica NZ 's Scott Base . <p> ? The starfish will be raised both " under present day conditions and under conditions predicted for the Antarctic at the end of the century , " Lamare explained in an Antarctica New Zealand programme summary . <p> Those scenarios predict seawater will be 2-3 degrees Celsius warmer and more acidic than today . The food made available to the starfish will also be a variable . <p> Starfish will be " sacrificed " at set intervals over 18 months to determine if and how they are adapting to different conditions . Many will procreate and these offspring will be sacrificed as well . <p> Lamare and colleagues will be testing these starfish 's " capacity to adapt " , he said in the summary . <p> One @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ simplified terms , it 's whether children can tolerate changed conditions better than their parents . More precisely , it 's whether " the offspring of parents subjected to changes ( ie : warmer conditions ) are more tolerant of the new environment " , Lamare said . <p> This is possible if a mother invests more resources into eggs to make them more resilient to environmental change . <p> Lamare and colleagues from Auckland , Sydney , Delaware and Brussels hypothesise that the research may conclude " polar species respond to climate change by producing more robust offspring " . <p> That 's right . This species of starfish may do better as a result of climate change . <p> Or , more precisely , some individuals will respond robustly and evolution will select them . In an interview , Lamare said over time the population could adapt to warmer and more acidic waters . <p> Transgenerational plasticity is also possible through a process called " epigenetics " . In simplified terms , it is genes expressing differently rather than a change in the genetic code itself . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ than changing . <p> Lamare 's team hope to show " all is not lost " in terms of climate change and that " biology has many tools available to persist in the face of climate change " . <p> This hopeful note does n't detract from the seriousness of climate change , but rather adds to the growing body of research showing climate change is complex and will produce winners and losers . <p> Nor does it predict the survival of this species of starfish . Other factors could yet contribute to their decline , including wider ecological changes , loss of sea ice and reduced food supply . <p> Meanwhile , Lamare is involved in a similar experiment with starfish from Australia 's Great Barrier Reef . Food is more abundant there compared with Antarctica but it 's possible starfish species from the reefs wo n't adapt as quickly to warming and acidifying seas . <p> Lamare got his PhD in marine science from Otago in 1997 and has visited Antarctica at least 16 times . His early work looked at the effects of ultraviolet radiation on @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ interested in the response of marine invertebrates to increases in sea temperature and whether warming of New Zealand oceans could see the expansion of warm-water species into northern New Zealand . <p> The Antarctic starfish he 's studying are " abundant , they travel well , they 're happy in the lab and we 've got lots of experience with them " . 
@@15730191 <h> The politics of Christchurch 's glass box rebuild <p> NICK SARGENT <p> Last updated 05:00 , December 5 2016 <p> Dean Kozanic <p> " To rebuild a broken city in glass might seem like giving the rolling ground a middle finger , but glass is a practical material . " <p> OPINION : The architecture of the rebuild should n't be dismissed as merely a passing style . Instead its materials and spaces speak of a particular politics . I think most people intuitively understand this and I suspect it 's why the city 's new glass boxes receive ongoing criticism . <p> But glass is easily cleaned ; greasy thumbprints have little effect . For this reason we need Christchurch 's architects -- people trained to understand cities and buildings -- to overcome their famous caution and speak openly about politics . <p> A building 's appearance matters . Its surfaces tell us about design decisions , and provide clues about its intended purpose . Through colour , texture and form , appearances also set a mood , perhaps inviting us to relax and spend time , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ and function are hard to separate . <p> Christchurch and Canterbury Tour <p> " Glass is ... transparent ; visually , obviously , but also politically . Glass buildings tell us the institutions and corporations who use them have nothing to hide . " <p> And if a building survives and its materials are allowed to age then it may also tell us its history . Without applying decoration -- although this can be done too -- a building 's appearance tells a story . So what do Christchurch 's new glass boxes have to say ? <p> " At its worst , much of Christchurch 's new architecture is big , blank and boring . " <p> To rebuild a broken city in glass might seem like giving the rolling ground a middle finger , but glass is a practical material . It is hard , durable and lets in light . Glass facades , being extremely thin , enlarge leasable floor area and thus rental income . They maintain visual openness while controlling access and noise . <p> The environmental friendliness of glass is debated , but @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 're told ( as though we had n't noticed ) , it is transparent ; visually , obviously , but also politically . Glass buildings tell us the institutions and corporations who use them have nothing to hide . <p> However , glass is more interesting than this simple story admits . First , it is n't always transparent . Reflections obscure views and in certain light create mirrors , often giving both passers by and occupants a feeling they are watched . <p> With added dark tints or permanent mirrors to control heat gain and loss , many glass buildings also have a visually heavy and sinister presence , even reflecting sunshine and making nearby spaces unpleasant . <p> Further , because methods of glass facade construction are big and simple , it can be difficult for designers to include detail , variety , or even windows so that occupants might control their own environment . <p> Importantly , glass also resists culture ; it does n't accept the dirt , marks or refurbishments that allow other materials , like brick or concrete or wood , to record @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ an amply and aptly neurotic investment in cleaning , stays young forever . <p> At its worst , much of Christchurch 's new architecture is big , blank and boring ; it rejects the physical and social context in which it sits , instead protecting the interests of powerful individuals and organisations . Its designers claim transparency and sociability , while deceptively creating hard , uninhabitable boundaries to defend private , profit-making space . <p> This should not be ignored . Locally , many still fight for house repairs or a voice in political decisions , whilst globally , climate change and wealth inequality escalate dangerously . To address these issues we need to discuss the politics of how we design our buildings and cities , and against this backdrop many of the new glass boxes look detached and insensitive . <p> This makes it important for the public -- the users of architecture -- to feel encouraged in expressing their opinions . It is an important conversation that the architectural profession is well-qualified to join but seem reluctant to support . <p> There are , of course , @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ the city 's carbon footprint , reinstating native wetlands , supplying affordable housing , creating more active social spaces , innovating with alternative construction financing and so on . While these political projects are exciting , their values are not clearly supported by most of the city 's new architecture . <p> How buildings look matters and do n't let the practically minded tell you otherwise . While the difficulty of design and construction often goes unappreciated , our political problems are too urgent to ignore . Planners , developers , architects and even politicians could approach these issues in open conversation . <p> Glass will be involved and , far from dull , lets hope it 's sharp and well directed . <p> Nick Sargent is a graduate architect and teacher who works in Christchurch and Sydney .

@@15737327 <p> OPINION : Wise men and fools have been predicting the end of the world from about the time someone somewhere came up with the idea that all good things must end . They never said why they must end but it sounded wise ... it probably was n't . <p> We know the popular image of a devout prophet , from any one of hundreds of religions , predicting the end of mankind as a punishment for our wickedness . <p> Placard in hand they preach to a non-listening world and then go home satisfied that at least they tried ... some of them are still trying . <p> Then there are those who use , or misuse , the current science to predict the demise of human life through rising temperatures and climate change brought about by over-population , the burning of fossils fuels , the destruction of forests , pollution of the air and oceans and the end of essential finite resources . <p> They forget that we are an adaptable lot with the ability change to solar energy as soon as the last oil well @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ stone-age ended long before we ran out of stones but we changed over to plastics and therein lies at least some of the problem . <p> Which of these prophets are wise or foolish depends on our understanding of the rationale they present to justify their predictions . Some rationale is clearly irrational and some can be disturbingly convincing . <p> American climate change specialist Guy McPherson has predicted that in 10 years humans will be extinct . He is on a national speaking tour in New Zealand and gave that disturbing message to a meeting of followers at the Wintec Campus in Hamilton last week . How many are still his followers is not known . <p> He has assured those who will listen to him that he is serious about that prediction . <p> It 's not the sort of prediction anyone should make who was not serious as the Kiwi sense of humour does not stretch that far . <p> While no one so far has put such an accurate time frame on the demise of humanity there is a general international acceptance that human activity @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ rely on and that alteration could prove fatal to us if not arrested . <p> Scientists also tell us that massive extinctions have occurred before in the very long history of the world . Whole ecosystems , millions of years old , simply disappeared following asteroid impacts . In our own very short lifetimes we know of dozens of life forms which have become extinct due to human activity and many more hover on the brink . <p> Humans have been around for only an eye blink in earth time and it has to be admitted we have stuffed things up in the short couple of hundred years since we developed the ability to do so . Before that we adapted our lifestyles to suit the environment but now we adapt the environment to suit our lifestyles and perhaps we need to change lifestyles before we can stop the rot . <p> Many of the world 's governments have agreed that something needs to be done but , as yet , can not agree what will be done , who will do it and , most importantly , who @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ makes fiddling while the capital city burns to the ground seem like urgent action . <p> There is also a significant body of opinion that suggests climate change , and all that flows from it , is nothing more than a giant international hoax so someone somewhere can make a whole lot of money ... it probably is n't . <p> Reactions have been varied , from hurling abuse at the news media for telling the story to undisguised contempt for the professor . It 's the job of the news media to tell these stories and up to us to decide how to react , if at all . So should we take the good professor 's prediction and time frame seriously ? Probably not but that does not mean we can ignore it all together . <p> Without doubt human activity has major negative impacts on the delicate environment we , and the wild things we share the world with , rely on to stay alive . <p> There needs to be a stronger international commitment to accurately define the problem and an equally strong commitment to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ longer than 10 years to take corrective action ... probably .

@@15748836 <h> Study : Warming to trigger 3 times as many downpours in US <p> WASHINGTON ( AP ) " Extreme downpours " like those that flooded Louisiana , Houston and West Virginia earlier this year " will happen nearly three times as often in the United States by the end of the century , and six times more frequently in parts of the Mississippi Delta , according to a new study . <p> Scientists have long pointed out that warmer air holds more moisture , so man-made climate change will increase the frequency of extreme downpours . That increase has already started , they say , but new work with much stronger computer simulations shows just how bad it will get , and where . <p> The high-resolution computer simulation " about 25 times better than other computer models " projects at least a fivefold increase in downpours in the Gulf Coast , Atlantic Coast and Southwest , according to a study in Monday 's journal Nature Climate Change . <p> Study lead author Andreas Prein , a scientist at the National Center for Atmospheric Research , said the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of downpours by 2100 . The Midwest and parts of the West Coast are expected to see the smallest increases . <p> Previous projections have n't been as detailed because they could not take into consideration small scale weather events like thunderstorms . The new computer simulations can , Prein said . <p> He looked at the type of thunderstorms that are in the top one half of 1 percent of rainmakers . <p> " It 's much more likely that you 'll get hit by very strong thunderstorms , very strong downpours in the future climate , " Prein said . " What this means in the future is you might have a much higher potential for flash floods . This can have really big impacts . " <p> Outside experts praised the study . <p> " The paper elegantly shows why these heavy downpours increase in frequency when the air is moist but decrease when the air is dry , " said Stanford University climate scientist Chris Field . " With high warming through the century , this paper projects that most of the U.S. ( gets @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 
@@15754418 <h> Kaikoura quake highlights need for Risk Agency <p> The recent devastating Kaikoura earthquake and aftershocks has once again brought into sharp relief the need for New Zealand to be better prepared for natural disasters . <p> Councils are often at the forefront of disaster response and recovery and Local Government New Zealand says a proposed " Risk Agency " will help equip them and their communities to respond to future upheavals . <p> Planning for a centralised local government Risk Agency to assist councils in understanding their risk profiles and work to a common set of standards has been underway for over a year . <p> LGNZ President Lawrence Yule says the proposed Agency would establish guidelines and models local government could use to manage risks and share information so councils could make better informed decisions on how to prepare for and respond to hazard events . <p> A business plan for the Risk Agency is with central government , as it requires seed funding for the setup and the initial task of a standardised assessment of all hazards throughout the country and the nature of the risk @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ guidance of Agency staff , would help each council make decisions on matters such as moving or altering infrastructure , planning for development , and buying insurance . <p> LGNZ has recently worked with local authorities to produce a guide to financial aspects of risk management , including when and how to purchase insurance and has begun work on a risk framework . This will provide local authorities with a common way of speaking about and applying risk assessment and management tools and concepts . <p> " Councils are generally well placed to understand what 's at risk and what their residents want and need . They are also best placed to lead recovery locally . But there is currently variation in the resources and planning skills available to each of them . The proposed Risk Agency along with collaboration with the Crown and other agencies aims to fix that . " <p> " Getting better at preparing means communities and the nation can recover from disasters more quickly , both economically and socially , " Mr Yule says . " We need to do this because New Zealand @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ tsunami , landslides and volcanic activity . The position statement aims to demonstrate local government 's commitment to achieving the vision for prosperous communities , and encourage actions that mandate and empower local government to make the most effective contribution to mitigating emissions and adapting to the effects of climate change . <p> " Councils and their communities will need to consider how rising sea levels and more frequent storms will impact them and the infrastructure they use , " Mr Yule says @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ impacts of climate change , often at a global level , but many remain uninformed about the impacts that climate change could have or are unaware of how the actions of their community can help mitigate emissions . <p> " Local government has a major role to play in helping us all adapt to climate change . The OECD notes that when faced with climate change impacts communities look to local government . Decisions made today about infrastructure , land use and urban development will determine the extent and impact of climate change , and a community 's vulnerability or resilience . " <p> To start this conversation LGNZ launched in July the 2050 Challenge discussion document , which highlights major economic , social , cultural and environmental shifts New Zealand will face in the next 35 years , including as a result of climate change . <p> The climate change position statement seeks to demonstrate local government 's commitment to tackling climate change adaptation , and encourage actions that mandate and empower local government to make the most effective contribution to mitigating emissions and adapting to the effects @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ local government alone will be just one part of a broader response and the position statement reflects this reality by considering how the decisions of other players can help local government decisions achieve good results . <p> " LGNZ continues to advocate for a forum for central and local government to discuss climate change issues , particularly in relation to areas where there is existing development and infrastructure investment , " Mr Yule says . <p> Prime Minister John Key has announced that he 's resigning as Prime Minister in seven days ' time . " In Leila 's words , " ' Love Cells ' represents the fact that we are all the same underneath -- we are cellular . Acts of bigotry , racial hatred , war and poor-blaming and future disregard of global poverty and climate change , shows how the belief that we are separate is dangerous . " <p> After premiering ' Love Cells ' via Afropunk , Leila successfully ran a crowdfunding campaign to tour her Love Cells and Scary Love Monster EP releases . Between December and February , Leila is bringing her ' Love Cells ' tour to New Zealand for four shows in Christchurch , Palmerston North , Wellington and Auckland . <p> Leila Adu @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ ' recorded by Steve Albini and ' Ode to the Unknown Factory Worker ' produced for the Italian National Radio . Based in Brooklyn , she is a currently a Princeton doctoral fellow and also teaches music to prisoners at Sing Sing Correctional Facility as a faculty member of Musicambia - music for social change . @@15756516 <h> Questions &amp; Answers - 6 November 2016 <p> 1 . DAVID BENNETT ( National -- Hamilton East ) to the Minister of Finance : What reports has he received confirming New Zealand 's sovereign credit rating ? <p> Hon BILL ENGLISH ( Minister of Finance ) : Late yesterday , following the Prime Minister 's announcement , rating agency Moody 's confirmed New Zealand 's triple A stable sovereign credit rating -- and I think the Prime Minister was slightly disappointed about that ! Moody 's said that New Zealand 's strong institutions support a smooth transition and policy continuity , that the New Zealand Government has effectively pursued a policy of fiscal consolidation and Government debt is likely to remain low , and that GDP growth has been strong and broad-based . <p> David Bennett : What reports has he received on the Government 's accounts showing tax revenues higher than forecast ? <p> Hon BILL ENGLISH : Earlier today the Government released its financial statements for the 4 months to October , which show that the operating balance is around $0.9 billion better than forecast in @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Crown tax revenue is $670 million higher , largely due to higher corporate tax revenues and GST . However , these numbers do not include the anticipated cost of the Kaikoura earthquake , but indicate that the economy is growing strongly enough that those costs can be met without a significant impact on the Government books . <p> David Seymour : When might the people who overpaid that extra tax get it back ? <p> Hon BILL ENGLISH : They will not be overpaying ; they will just be paying what is legally due , although , as I sometimes point out , there is no legal maximum to how much tax you can pay -- you are free to pay more if you want to , and it will be gratefully accepted -- but what the numbers do show is that the economy is a bit stronger than was expected back in Budget 2016 . <p> David Seymour : So when will the Government change those laws so it does not tax people more than it requires for its expenditure ? <p> Hon BILL ENGLISH : The nature @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ between the National Party and the ACT Party . Where we are collecting more than we need , then there is the possibility of reducing taxes . <p> David Bennett : How is debt tracking against forecasts , as reported in today 's Crown financial statements ? <p> Hon BILL ENGLISH : Debt is tracking a little bit better than forecast . We have low Government debt by international comparisons . It peaked at about 25 or 26 percent of GDP , and it is gradually dropping -- and these accounts indicate it is dropping a bit faster than we expected . <p> Rt Hon Winston Peters : In respect of the finance Minister 's discussions and negotiations with the ACT Party , on what day , and what address , and in which telephone booth did those negotiations take place ? <p> Mr SPEAKER : I will leave it to the Minister , if he wants to answer -- the Hon Bill @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ many years the member 's reference to the meetings that he does conduct in telephone booths , but these ones are conducted in the Prime Minister 's office because ACT is part of the governing coalition of New Zealand -- and New Zealand First is not . <p> David Seymour : Is the Minister aware that history has moved on and most people actually have cellphones now ? <p> Hon BILL ENGLISH : Yes . <p> David Bennett : How does lower debt and ongoing discipline in spending support resilience in the New Zealand economy ? <p> Hon BILL ENGLISH : The lessons of the Kaikoura earthquake , I think , demonstrate exactly why reducing Government debt maintains our resilience . This is an earthquake that could cost $2 billion to $3 billion . If the books were in worse shape , that would prevent us making other positive choices we could make to invest in transport and infrastructure , but because the books are in good shape we should be able to absorb the cost of the earthquake and continue to invest in infrastructure . <p> Does he @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ have changed the flag " , when asked for his greatest regret ? <p> Rt Hon JOHN KEY ( Prime Minister ) : Yes , but the member should know that I also stated that my other regrets were that we were unable to establish the Kermadec Ocean Sanctuary and that the Trans-Pacific Partnership had not been fully ratified . Of course , there are many other things that I would love to continue working on , but I will be leaving those for the future leadership team . <p> Metiria Turei : Does the Prime Minister regret leaving 85,000 New Zealand children living in severe hardship ? <p> Rt Hon JOHN KEY : I am very proud of the record that this Government has established and maintained for the most vulnerable young New Zealanders . New Zealand will be building tens of thousands of houses over the next few years . @@15766948 <p> OPINION : It seems incontrovertible that the global future will be affected by the sequence of democratic choices made in Europe and America in the second part of 2016 . A flood of opinion and prophecy engulfs us currently about the causes and the effects . Yet It is far too early to predict with confidence the full consequences whether for New Zealand or for the world more generally . <p> These 2016 convulsions occur at a time of unprecedented change in the relative economic weight among important economies in the world . Despite a destabilising 2008/09 global financial crisis , emerging economies , particularly China and others in Asia , have proven largely resilient , although previously remarkable growth rates have slowed . <p> It is essential that China and other major emerging nations accept principled responsibility to play a constructive and equitable part in the management of the rules-based international system . Yet the United States and other established powers have , to this point , proved reluctant to concede modifications to the pecking order fashioned by them in earlier times . <p> The 2016 trans-Atlantic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ emerging economies frustrated at delay are themselves now devising instruments for financial and economic co-operation . That feeds concern in Washington and elsewhere that newcomers are intent upon changing established order . <p> Opportunities conferred upon NZ by this shift in global economic gravity now define substantially the country 's modern dependency . While retaining authentic global trade and other interests , regionalism in Asia Pacific is paramount . American engagement in the region is important , although this is not necessarily the same thing as leadership , at least in East Asia . Leadership is either bestowed or it is asserted . In East Asia several governments collectively cherish leadership of their own region , even as they welcome US engagement . <p> Negotiations proceed about deeper and wider regional economic co-operation involving all East Asian governments ( and NZ ) but where US is absent . Obviously NZ can not stand aside from such a process even as we officially regret American repudiation of the sub-regional and sub-optimal Trans-Pacific Partnership . NZ 's present favourable economic situation has been secured in the absence of formal trade ties @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ effort . Yet any American relapse into greater protectionism would , with its wide knock-on effects , be a cause for serious alarm . <p> The 2016 democratic choices in Europe and America are symptoms of misgiving about consequences domestically of unregulated free market globalisation . We may be at the threshold of some serious second thoughts about free market wisdom . Likewise , fears about a surrender of national decision-making to unaccountable regional or global authorities also explain the outcomes . Pressures on established or prospective international arrangements about trade , climate change , arms control and disarmament , people movement , poverty reduction and refugees are therefore clearly on the cards . <p> The US presidential result promises that impulsive personality will be a larger factor now in international relations . Experience over the past 30 years or more illustrates NZ can operate successfully beneath the radar screens of the powerful as " a friend but not formal ally " of any major power ; while remaining committed to international rules-based order for great and small alike . It is essential to strive to retain this judicious @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ be simple and on different issues NZ may find itself keeping different and non-traditional company . <p> Properly resourced NZ diplomacy will be essential alongside those security/intelligence capabilities that have received such prominence and additional resource from government in recent times , with American encouragement . Diplomatic judgment will be at a premium as NZ navigates a post-2016 world . Things will get harder if " making America strong again " in Washington entails yet greater US self-exemption from international rules or norms of behaviour on trade , the environment , human rights , international justice or war fighting . NZ 's sense of shared values with the US may decline . Whether a distracted post-Brexit Europe will be as concerned with boosting equitable international rules , is likewise at issue . <p> Finally , 2016 has been notable for the NZ presence on the UN Security Council ( UNSC ) as a non permanent member . That is a comparatively rare experience and involved a considerable effort to win a place . As the tenure ends there are inevitably questions about whether it was all worth the effort @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ This is the wrong question . Rather it is what influence does council membership now have upon NZ ? First-hand exposure over two years to disappointment and frustration at council deadlock because of great power discord ( on Syria , the Middle East peace process etc ) reinforces the absolute necessity for independent NZ judgment . Fallout from 2016 decisions inside Europe and America now places even greater premium on further refining that judgment . 
@@15767794 <h> Charles Firth : There could be just two years left before the North Pole disappears <p> Over the past few weeks the temperature of the North Pole has been 22 degrees hotter than the average temperature for this time of year . Photo / 123rf <p> My 6-year-old asked me about Santa the other day . Luckily , it was n't the moment where his innocence is shattered forever . <p> Instead , he was wondering how Santa was going , preparing for his annual voyage around the world , dispensing plastic junk from China to all the world 's least-needy kids . ( I added the last part , but you get the drift ) . <p> I painted the picture that my parents had passed on to me . I explained to him how the night is slowly descending across the North Pole at the moment , and by the time Santa sets off on his sleigh across the ice on Christmas Eve , it will be shrouded in continuous darkness , lit only by his Christmas candles , and one shiny red nose . <p> @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ talked at length about Santa 's warm red jacket . The sad thing that I did n't have the heart to tell my son is that , at the moment , Santa 's big red jacket is probably too warm for Santa himself , even at the North Pole . <p> Over the past few weeks the temperature of the North Pole has been 22 degrees hotter than the average temperature for this time of year . That 's not a typo . It 's not 2.2 degrees hotter . It 's 22 degrees Celsius hotter . <p> The reason it 's such a huge difference is because even though night is now falling , the temperature around the poles is still getting hotter rather than colder . That 's never happened before . What it means is that the gap between average temperature and this year 's temperature is getting wider and wider by the day . <p> Here is the graph that Zack Labe , a PhD student at Cornell University , who first discovered it , put out a couple of weeks ago : <p> The @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ norm for the past 100,000 years or so . That lonely red line that started going down , but then decided to start going up again , is this year 's temperature . <p> There are a lot of implications for this . In salt water , minus five degrees ( which is the current air temperature in the North Pole ) is too warm for ice to form . That means the planet 's natural airconditioner ( and sun reflector ) is not regrowing back this winter . Two of the ways the earth keeps itself cool have just broken down . <p> When it comes to climate , I 'm a pretty lousy citizen . I do all the things you 're not supposed to do . I use a clothes dryer , I eat red meat , I even stopped offsetting my energy bills when I got my new mortgage . <p> It 's not that I 'm a climate sceptic - far from it . But I 've become immune to reports about the gradual heating of the planet . I know I supposed to @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ degrees hotter than usual , but it 's hard to get that worked up about it . It just feels too intangible . <p> But 22 degrees is different . This moment has long been theorised , and now it 's here . This is the awful moment where the feedback loops start feeding into each other in a big way . <p> The ice caps melt because it 's getting warmer and then because the ice caps have melted , the earth gets even warmer even faster . <p> Peter Wadhams , a professor of Ocean Physics at Cambridge University , now reckons that it could be as little as two years before ice disappears completely from the North Pole during the summer months . <p> I know this next bit is obvious , but I 'm going to point it out anyway , because I did n't quite realise it until it was pointed out to me . What the ice melting at the North Pole means is that , once it 's gone , you 'll be able to drive a boat across the North Pole @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Pole , doing a cheesy thumbs in front of a sign saying " North Pole " . No more stories of Santa 's sleigh . There will be no ice for him to drive his sleigh across . <p> In addition to the environmental implications , the cultural implications of this are also huge . <p> Polar bears are among the animals most affected by changes in Arctic sea ice because they rely on this surface for essential activities like hunting , travelling and breeding . Photo / 123rf <p> We will need to start changing the stories that we tell ourselves and our children about what our planet looks like . The idea that ice was once at the North Pole will become folklore , much like the fabled North-West passage through the Arctic , which as recently as a decade ago was considered treacherous and impassable , but has now become a common route for ships and tourist boats in the summer months . <p> This is why I 'm seriously considering taking the kids out of school next July , and taking them up to the @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ last chance to see it . Fantasies of the North Pole are such a vivid part of my own childhood . When was a kid , I always assumed I 'd go there one day . Now I know my children will not . <p> If I do take my kids to see the North Pole and they live long lives , they 'll end be some of the last people on earth to be able to attest that there was indeed , ice at the top of our planet . <p> I know I should probably explain some of this to my son . I know I probably should n't be passing on the myths that my parents passed on to me . But in the same way I ca n't bring myself to break it to him that Santa does n't exist , I also do n't have the heart to break it to him that climate change does . For that is a shattering of his innocence that he 'll be dealing with for the rest of his life . <p> Charles Firth majored in Economic @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ editor of The Chaser Quarterly . Follow him on Twitter @charlesfirth 
@@15770803 <h> From New York to the South Pole : producing TEDxScottBase <p> When experts and celebrities gather for the first ever Antarctic TEDx event hosted by a government research station in January , they will be relying on the technical acumen of pretty much one man . <p> Isaac Spedding is absolutely the right person for the job . Currently employed at Ara Institute of Canterbury 's New Zealand Broadcasting School ( NZBS ) , Spedding 's passion for broadcasting , and talent for troubleshooting , has taken him from the fashion runways of the world to the secret technical corners of 1 Times Square , New York , and now to South Pole . <p> Spedding will film TEDxScottBase on Sunday 15th January 2017 in Antarctica , which will then be presented and webcast in the NZBS studios to a global audience on 22st January . TEDxScottBase will be one of a number of events to mark the start of the 60th anniversary of Scott Base , New Zealand 's presence in Antarctica since 1957 . <p> The bare essentials <p> Scott Base , New Zealand 's only @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ biggest of these is technical kit . The venue , the Hillary Field Centre , has virtually no AV equipment -- in a place where bare essentials are transported in and out , an early suggestion of hanging a sheet as a projector screen was only half made in jest . <p> So , a cargo allowance of around 1100 pounds , makes for very careful packing . <p> Luckily , Spedding is nothing if not resourceful . " The whole show design has gone through seven revisions ... a lot of planning has gone into getting to where we are now , which is a completely 3D pre-visualisation of the room and a trial run of all the equipment . <p> " We started designing the show with the traditional technology , so a big mixer , lights , cameras and a projector with a screen , as you would see anywhere else . Then because of logistics we have been forced to be very creative , so we 're taking 36 fluorescent lighting tubes and we 're going to remove all their tubes and put our ones @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ 're going to have beautiful colour correction fluro tubes specifically to light the show . That 's the lightest way I could figure out to light the scene with full colour rendering . It 's nerdy , but for under $500 we change the look , and it 's that little bit better . " <p> The benefits of nerdy <p> Largely self-taught , Spedding spent a year " nerding out " in the studios of the NZBS practising editing and animation skills through the Digital Video Post Production programme . We had 12 cameras all around the country that we could control remotely from the studio in New York so I was responsible for those , like the Knitting Factory in Brooklyn , a very popular event centre , and we had these futuristic robot cameras that you could control with a joystick back at home . It was my responsibility to go in there and fix them . So I had key card access to 1 @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ to the roof of Times Square and the only other people allowed up there were the roof layers ! " <p> In the States he produced live webcasts on equipment hired by his employer Livestream at short notice -- he would read 100-plus page manuals on the plane and then train the rest of the team to use the equipment when he arrived . <p> " The production life is like being a celebrity without all the attention , so it 's kind of great . You get the travel , you get the experiences , you get to hang out at the parties . " The fashion world was all that and more : " It 's everything that you expect : long hours , big parties , high stress . Funny stories . It 's great and you work with Italians and people from everywhere . " <p> Staying grounded <p> Despite the jetset lifestyle , he had no trouble staying grounded ( " you do n't get caught up , especially if you 're from New Zealand " ) and found ways to de-stress . Skiing @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ eventually the gloss wore off . <p> " You spend so much time working in very high end commercial world that you kind of achieve all of those goals quite quickly and now I am a lot more socially conscious . <p> " At the start you want all the bells and whistles . You want to work for these brands because they 're cool . You go and do that stuff because it is worth a lot of money , and you want to meet these people because they are the top of the field in fashion or something . But once you 've done that you start thinking about what is more important and needs more attention or what is actually doing good rather than not . " <p> Which brings us back to TEDxScottBase , where climate change is foremost in scientists ' minds . <p> Changing attitudes towards climate change <p> " I think now more than ever it is so important to have events such as these and to try to make people aware of the climate of the world . <p> building resilience through labour mobility ; <p> migration and displacement as they relate to loss and damage under the Warsaw International Mechanism on Loss and Damage ; <p> and regional mechanisms to address the needs of migrants and displaced persons . @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ funded PCCM project which aims to develop the capacity of Pacific Island countries to address the impacts of climate change on migration through well-managed , rights-based migration schemes and policy frameworks , supported by comprehensive research and knowledge building . <p> It is a joint collaboration between the European Union funded Pacific Climate Change Migration Project ( PCCM ) implemented by the United Nations Economic and Social Commission for Asia and the Pacific ( UNESCAP ) , the International Labour Organisation ( ILO ) , the United Nations Development Program ( UNDP ) and the Pacific Islands Forum Secretariat ( PIFS ) with support from International Organisation for Migration ( IOM ) and the United Nations University ( UNU ) . <p> ' Highly disruptive ' Delivering the keynote address at the opening of the meeting , Tuvalu Prime Minister Enele Sosene Sopoaga said : " Climate change displacement and unplanned relocation are highly disruptive to livelihoods , culture and society and require proper , well-planned interventions to support people in their efforts to adapt to the challenges , particularly in securing access to decent livelihoods . <p> " @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ of primary concern to Pacific Islanders in any form of climate change-related migration . <p> " The international response must also include adequate strategies to deal with persons displaced because of climate change , and their human rights must be protected . " <p> Speaking on behalf of the European Union , Christoph Wagner said : " It is clear that climate change , and the impact climate change has on the environment , will become an increasingly important driver of migration from rural to urban areas within Pacific island countries and to other countries . <p> " The European Union is supporting the PCCM project to help prepare our partner countries for migration . Those who are going to be leaving their countries , either temporarily or on a permanent basis , need assistance from their governments , Pacific regional organisations and development partners . <p> " We also want to help those Pacific island countries who are going to be receiving migrants to maximise the opportunities that the additional labour , expertise and experience can offer . This must include those who do not want to move " <p> The UN Resident Coordinator for the Pacific based in Fiji , Osnat Lubrani said the UN considers this complex issue requires greater attention in the context of the Pacific region 's journey to achieve the 2030 Sustainable Development Agenda . <p> The head of UNESCAP Pacific Office , Iosefa Maiava , noted that the need to address climate change and mobility issues is recognised in the newly-adopted Framework for Resilient Development in the Pacific ( FRDP ) by Pacific leaders . <p> The regional meeting will build on existing global and regional policy directions to promote alignment and coherence , including the FRDP , the Paris Agreement , the Warsaw International Mechanism on Loss and @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ @ Agenda for Sustainable Development .
